Rev 3.41: and `changes`, `[{field, from, to}]` for each of `fact_key`, `aliases`, `tags`, `valid_from`, `valid_to` whose values differ from the target's, the values as lists; the Memory view draws one row per field, `openalpaca memory suggestions` a `changes:` line under the suggestion's, and the count of hidden characters includes the values. `reason` is none for the chat model's own suggestion, whose body may copy the target's old `Replaces …` sentence.)
  - It reads the index (`note.supersedes`, LR18) and verifies each listed file, as recall does. It parses no partition.
  - The list is newest first. Where several suggestions name one target, only the newest is listed, and `older_count` says how many were hidden.
  - `POST /v1/memories/{id}` with `action: confirm` takes `target_revision` in the body. It is **required** when the candidate carries `supersedes` (`400 MEMORY_INVALID_INPUT` otherwise) and checked by S5. The result gains `superseded` and `dismissed`.
  - `POST /v1/memories/{id}/undo` also accepts a candidate (§3.3).
  - `docs/api/` is regenerated.
- **GUI** (`apps/openalpaca-gui/src/views/memory/`):
  - **`Suggestions.tsx`** sits above `RecentlyLearned` in `MemoryView.tsx`. Each row reads "Replace “X” (saved by you) with “B”?", shows **both texts** (X's excerpt and B's knowledge), and gives the reason and "From what you said on <date>". Both texts matter because §2.5 case 3 can raise a suggestion that only restates X.
    - **Replace** sends confirm with `If-Match` set to B's revision and `target_revision`.
    - **Keep mine** sends `POST …/{B}/undo` with `If-Match` set to B's revision: a withdrawal, which forgets nothing else the user said (§3.3).
    - The toasts read "Replaced — “X” is kept as history" and "Kept your note".
    - The section hides when empty.
  - **Candidate copy follows the origin** (`views/memory/copy.ts:41-43`, which says "not said by you" today): an `extracted_user` suggestion reads "From what you said — would replace “X”". *(Rev 3.33: one the chat model saved — `extracted_user` with no `observed_at`, LQ14's datum — reads "Suggested by Alpaca — would replace “X”", and its Suggestions row "Suggested by Alpaca on <date>"; `openalpaca memory suggestions` says "suggested by the assistant". Rev 3.34: the origin label in a row's subline, the detail's meta row and *Where this came from* stays per origin — `extracted_user` reads "Learned from what you said" whoever wrote it, as `memory_store`'s description says the note is recorded — because a list item carries no `observed_at`; the suggestion's own wording says who suggested it.)*
  - **A replacement row in *recently learned*** reads "Learned from what you said · replaced “A” · Undo". Its undo toast reads "Undone — “A” is back".
  - New hooks `useSuggestions`, `useConfirmSuggestion` and `useDismissSuggestion` (`hooks/useMemories.ts`), and a new query key `qk.memory.suggestions()`. `memory_changed` already invalidates `["memory"]` (`lib/query-client.ts:181-182`).
  - DESIGN_SPEC §5.7 gains the section.
- **CLI.**
  - `openalpaca memory suggestions [--global|--project <p>] [--limit N]` prints `<id8>  replace “X” (saved by you) → “B”  from your words, 2026-09-24 — <reason>`.
  - `openalpaca memory confirm <id>` fetches the candidate and, when it carries `supersedes`, its target's current revision. It sends both and prints what was replaced and dismissed.
  - `openalpaca memory undo <id>` dismisses a suggestion (§3.3). `openalpaca memory forget <id>` keeps its meaning.
  - `docs/CLI_Manual.md` gains these.

---

## 4. Confirming a suggestion in chat (MD-12)

### 4.1 The mechanism: the model asks, and the human's card answers

**Rejected — Option N, a card the daemon raises when extraction finds a suggestion.** Five reasons:
- MD-10's triggers fire when the user is idle, switching away or stopping, so the card would arrive outside any turn. With no `stream_id` it reaches no SSE, and the CLI's `chat` never sees it (`apps/openalpaca/src/chat_stream/mod.rs:436-441`, `apps/openalpacad/src/event_bridge.rs:443-452`).
- A card a window accepts blocks that window's composer (`apps/openalpaca-gui/src/lib/chat-stream.ts:313-315`). Every window accepts a `lane_key: null` card (`views/chat/useChatSession.ts:118-133`).
- A shutdown flush cannot wait for an answer.
- The request is tool-shaped (`crates/openalpaca_core/src/security/confirmation.rs:17-34`), so every consumer would print a pseudo-tool.
- It does not match MD-12's "the assistant may raise a suggested replacement".

**Adopted — Option T.** A new builtin **`memory_confirm`**, in `tools/builtins/memory_tools.rs`:

| Property | Type | Required | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `id` | uuid | yes | the suggestion: a candidate of origin `extracted_user` with non-empty `supersedes` and at least one user-authored cited row (§4.2 rule 5) |
| `expected_revision` | revision | yes | the suggestion's revision, as listed |
| `target_revision` | revision | yes | the target's revision, as listed (S5) |

- **Result:** `{ "id", "revision", "status": "active", "superseded": [uuid], "dismissed": [uuid] }`. Refusals: `revision_conflict`, `not_found`, `invalid_input` (a target no longer in force — nothing is left to replace — or a malformed revision; rev 3.23, as built in `memory/service/in_chat.rs`: `suggestion_preview` and `check_revisions`), `not_a_suggestion` and `approval_required`; all but `approval_required` before any card. The schema is closed (`additionalProperties: false`).
- **Annotations:** `destructive_local_annotations()`, so it prompts on an attended turn and is refused unattended (`tools/builtins/mod.rs:587-599`).
- **Where it is offered.** Only on the **main loop's per-request surface** (`tools/builtins/main_loop.rs:164-190`), and only when the turn is attended and `ctx.source == Some("gui")`, the derived source the GUI and CLI chat share (`chat/service.rs:193`, `gateway/router/mod.rs:511-520`). It is never offered on connector lanes, unattended turns, the skill tier, the lead or subagents (ruling LR13).
- **Its description** tells the model to call it only for a suggestion listed in `<memory_suggestions>` (§4.5) on this turn, when the conversation reaches the topic or a natural pause, at most once per turn: once its card has been shown, whatever the answer, that suggestion and every other suggestion for the same note leave the list in every conversation on that chat channel (the lane) until OpenAlpaca restarts, so it never raises one that is not listed now, even one it saw earlier (rev 3.22, residue R3: the rule is per lane, `MemoryService::mark_raised`, not per conversation). An inferred suggestion (origin `extracted_other`) is never listed there, and the tool refuses one with `not_a_suggestion`, naming the Memory view and `openalpaca memory confirm`.

`memory_update action: confirm` stays refused from a tool (G2). Today it raises a card and then refuses after the click (`builtins/mod.rs:596`, `partition.rs:1061-1068`), which wastes the click. §4.4's hook now refuses it **before** any card, with a message naming `memory_confirm` for a suggestion and the Memory view otherwise.

### 4.2 Why the human's Approve, and not the model's call, mints `user`

The sandbox can let a prompted call through with no click at all:
- on an approval-cache hit (`security/sandbox/mod.rs:274-281`);
- under `auto_approve` (`:282-307`), which comes from `[security] auto_approve_confirmations` (`orchestrator/query_handler/simple_query_handler.rs:335`);
- and "Always allow" caches the whole tool (`security/confirmation.rs:198-208`).

So "the tool ran after a prompt" proves nothing. Six rules make the click the only thing that can confirm:

1. **The approval is a fact only one branch creates.** `ToolContext` gains `human_approval: Option<HumanApproval { request_id: String }>` (`tools/registry/mod.rs:57`).
   - `SandboxManager::execute_tool` sets it **only** in the `ConfirmationResolution::Answered(resp) if resp.approved` branch (`sandbox/mod.rs:401-411`).
   - It writes it onto the per-call context it finalizes before dispatch (`:460-470`), and **always overwrites** the field there. A caller that builds a context with it set gets `None`.
   - It is never set on the cache-hit, `auto_approve`, unattended or no-broker branches (`:274-341`, `:427-441`).
   - The model cannot supply it: tool schemas are closed, and `ToolContext` is not derived from arguments.
2. **The approval is per call and never reused (ruling LR14).**
   - `tools/builtins/mod.rs` gains `approval_is_per_call(name) -> bool`, which is true only for `memory_confirm`. It sits beside `annotations_for_builtin` and is static per tool name, like every annotation (03 §7).
   - For such a tool the sandbox **skips the cache-hit and `auto_approve` branches** and **never records** into `ApprovalCache` (`sandbox/mod.rs:408-409`). An owner who set `auto_approve_confirmations` still gets the card: approving everything blindly must not mint `user` notes.
   - `ToolConfirmationRequested` carries `per_call: true`, so the GUI offers no **Always allow** (`components/chat/Composer.tsx:291-303`) and the CLI refuses `--entire-tool` for it (`commands/confirmations.rs:62-78`). The server ignores the scope anyway.
3. **Only a user-directed channel can deliver the Approve.** The card rides the turn's `{owner}:gui` lane. Connectors queue only confirmations whose lane ends in their own suffix (`crates/openalpaca_connectors/src/telegram/connector.rs:143-151`, `discord/connector.rs:234-240`, `imessage/connector.rs:198-204`), and their `/yes` pops only their own chat's queue (`common/mod.rs:110-172`). So the one way to answer is `POST /v1/chat/confirmations/{id}`, which builds the response server-side (`apps/openalpacad/src/routes/chat.rs:873-890`). The callers are the GUI card, the CLI's inline `[y/N]` and `openalpaca tasks confirmations approve`. That is the HTTP API, the CLI and the GUI, which is exactly 03 §4's user-directed surface.
4. **The args bind what the human saw.** `args_hash` covers `id`, `expected_revision` and `target_revision` (`sandbox/mod.rs:268`). The preview (§4.4) is computed from exactly those revisions, and the service re-checks both (S5).
5. **The service maps only this one act.** `MemoryService::confirm_suggestion(ctx, approval: &HumanApproval, id, expected_revision, target_revision)` requires:
   - a `ModelTool` context;
   - an approval;
   - a candidate that carries `supersedes`, of origin **`extracted_user`**, with at least one cited `message` row that is user-authored (§1.2; one indexed lookup of the cited ids).

   Anything else is `not_a_suggestion`. An inferred candidate (origin `extracted_other`: text from assistant replies and tools) is confirmable only in the Memory view and the CLI, so an in-chat card never presents the assistant's inference as the user's words. S10 keeps a model tool from editing a suggestion, so the text the card shows is the one its writer wrote. *(Rev 3.34: since LQ11 the chat model's own replacement of a saved note is also a suggestion of origin `extracted_user` citing the turn's typed row (§3.1 row 5), so it qualifies — and its text is the chat model's, not the extractor's. The card and the `<memory_suggestions>` line therefore say who wrote it: "suggested by the assistant on <date>" in place of "from what you said on <date>", told apart by LQ14's datum, `extracted_user` with no `observed_at` (`in_chat::by_the_assistant`). That a listed suggestion is raised on a later turn is the tool description's rule, not enforced: `memory_confirm` also accepts the ids `memory_store` returned in the same turn, and the Approve on the card is still required either way. Rev 3.35: rule 5 reads "a suggestion that cites a message the user typed", and the model-facing refusals say so — `not_a_suggestion` no longer speaks of "the user's own words", and `memory_update`'s confirm calls it "a suggestion waiting for the user". An edit's suggestion may cite only the copied note's rows (§3.1 row 5), so it qualifies also on a turn with no typed row of its own.)*

   It performs the confirm as `Actor::UserSurface` for that one write, so origin is `user` and `memory_changed.surface` is `"user"`. It then runs S6's dismissals, as withdrawals after the write. Every other call from the same context stays `ModelTool`: `archive` of a `user` note is still `authority_conflict` (`partition.rs:1078-1107`), and since LQ11 an `edit` or a `store` replacing one is a suggestion (§3.1 row 5) — never a write in force (rev 3.34).
6. **The model saying yes changes nothing.** Without the fact, `memory_confirm` answers `approval_required` and writes nothing. This is what a turn run with `auto_approve` would reach, if rule 2 did not already prevent it.

03 §4's definition therefore gains one sentence (§11): *a confirm of a suggestion whose approval is the human's Approve on the confirmation card for that exact call, delivered through `POST /v1/chat/confirmations/{id}`, is a user-directed act.*

### 4.3 Deny, timeout, cancel, unattended, connectors

- **Deny** (recommended answer to LQ2): the suggestion is **dismissed durably**.
  - `BuiltInTool` gains an optional hook, `on_confirmation_denied(&self, args, ctx)`, whose default does nothing (`tools/registry/mod.rs:222-230`).
  - The sandbox calls it in the `Answered` not-approved branch, before returning `Err` (`sandbox/mod.rs:412-416`), with `ctx.human_denial = Some(..)` set under rule 1's discipline.
  - `memory_confirm` implements it as a **withdrawal** of the candidate (§3.3): no links and no suppressed sources, so what else the same message taught stays learned. The model gets "The user declined; the suggestion was dismissed. Do not raise it again." X is untouched.
  - If the owner picks LQ2(b), the hook is not built. Deny then leaves the candidate in the Memory view, and the lane's `raised` set (§4.5) keeps it out of this conversation.
- **Timeout** (`confirmation_timeout_secs`, 300 s) and **Cancelled**: the candidate is left as it is, and the model gets the sandbox's existing message (`sandbox/mod.rs:417-425`). The suggestion is not raised again on that lane until the daemon restarts (§4.5).
- **Unattended turns** (scheduled skills, piped or one-shot `chat`, flagged follow-ups): `memory_confirm` is not on the surface. The sandbox would refuse it anyway (`sandbox/mod.rs:308-340`). The suggestion waits in the Memory view and the CLI, which is MD-12's "the Memory view and the CLI keep it too".
- **Connectors** (Telegram, Discord, iMessage): the tool is not offered and `<memory_suggestions>` is not rendered. A connector's `/yes` is keyed by chat and not by sender, and may land in a group (MD-4). It is not a user-directed surface by 03 §4's definition (G2). Connector-only users see suggestions in the app's Memory view.

### 4.4 The preview on the card

Today the card, the CLI prompt and the connector prompt show the literal arguments (`components/chat/ToolConfirmationBanner.tsx:9-10`, `chat_stream/mod.rs:462-475`). For `memory_confirm` those are three opaque ids. A title passed as an argument would be model-authored and could misdescribe the note. So the daemon writes the text.

- **Hook.** `BuiltInTool` gains `confirmation_preview(&self, args, ctx) -> Result<Option<String>, String>`, whose default is `Ok(None)`. The sandbox calls it in the broker branch before `broker.request` (`sandbox/mod.rs:341-359`):
  - `Err(reason)` returns `Err(reason)` with **no card**;
  - `Ok(Some(text))` attaches the preview.
- **`memory_confirm`'s preview.** It validates the suggestion and the target at the given revisions under §4.2 rule 5's conditions, and on a mismatch or a non-suggestion it refuses before any card. It renders at most 1 KiB, from the service's own read:

  ```text
  Replace your saved note “Editor preference” with “Editor preference”?
  Yours: I use vim for everything.
  New: Since June 2026 the user edits with Helix; before that, vim.
  Why: The user said they switched from vim to Helix in June. (from what you said on 2026-09-24)
  Your note is kept as history in the Memory view.
  ```

  The date in "(from what you said on …)" is the `created_at` of the newest **user-authored** row the suggestion cites, looked up from its `sources`, never fixed text. Rule 5 guarantees there is one. *(Rev 3.34: for a suggestion the chat model wrote (rule 5's note) the parenthesis reads "(suggested by the assistant on …)", and a preview with no reason ends its fourth line "Suggested by the assistant on ….", with the same date. Rev 3.35: for that suggestion the date is the day it was saved (`in_chat::said_on`), as the Memory view and the CLI date it — its newest cited typed row may be the older row of the note an edit copies. Rev 3.41: that suggestion gives no reason, so the line after "New:" (or after "Changes:") always reads "Suggested by the assistant on …." *(rev 3.42 corrects rev 3.41's "its fourth line")*; and every preview whose suggestion changes the note's key, aliases, tags or validity carries a line after "New:" — `Changes: tags none → tools; valid until none → 2026-12-31T00:00:00Z` — within 160 bytes, whose bytes the two texts give up (87 each) so the bound and the tail hold; `the_preview_keeps_its_tail_with_a_changes_line_at_its_worst_case` pins it. Rev 3.42: the 160 bytes were one cut in the wire's order, validity last, so a long alias list pushed an end date off the card; now every changed field keeps its label and "…", the validity window is given its bytes first and always fits whole, and the key and the lists share the rest, a short one whole before a long one is cut.)*

  **As built since the approval-surface hidden-character work (rev 3.24, 3.25; the spec's amendments A18, A19, A22, A32):**
  - **What it quotes.** The five fields the Memory view's Suggestions card serves (§3.5): the two titles and the reason whole, the replaced note's text as its **600-byte excerpt** (`target_excerpt`, quoted after "Yours:" or "Current:") and the suggestion's as its **4 KiB knowledge** (`suggested_knowledge`). A quote the daemon's own cut shortened ends with "…" even where what is left fits the preview's budget. Each field is cut to its own budget: titles 80 bytes, the reason 160, each text 260.
  - **What it drops.** Every character of the approval surfaces' escape set, `utils::text::needs_escape` (control, format, default-ignorable, unassigned and private-use code points, every blank but the plain space, U+2800, U+FFFC), through `utils::text::one_line`: the preview stays OpenAlpaca's prose, composed to be read.
  - **The count line.** When the five fields held any such character, a last line says so, counted over the whole of each served field by `memory::service::suggestion_hidden` — the count the Suggestions card and `openalpaca memory suggestions` show: `This preview leaves out N hidden character(s); the Memory view shows them as <U+…>.`
  - **The budget under the count.** With the count line present the two texts take 216 bytes each instead of 260 (`PREVIEW_TEXT_BYTES_UNDER_COUNT`), so the rest is at most 925 bytes for the longest wording ("your note (edited by hand)") and no cut reaches the date, the last line or the count at any count below 10^16; a preview that left nothing out is unchanged. `the_preview_keeps_its_tail_at_its_worst_case` pins that case.

- **`memory_update`'s preview.** With `action: confirm` it returns `Err(confirm_refused …)`, which fixes the wasted click (§4.1).
- **Wire.**
  - `ConfirmationRequest`, `SystemEvent::ToolConfirmationRequested`, `ServerEvent::ToolConfirmationRequested`, SSE `ConfirmationRequested` and `PendingConfirmation` each gain optional `preview` and `per_call` (`events.rs:338-370`; `crates/openalpaca_api/src/events/mod.rs:239-266`; `chat/stream_manager/mod.rs:58`; `routes/chat_types.rs:172-189`). The bridge's and persistence's patterns already end in `..` (`event_bridge.rs:420-429`, `events/persistence.rs:301-306`). The literals that build these events, and the one pattern without `..` (the SSE serializer, `routes/chat.rs:604-615`), are listed in §8.10.
  - **`event_log` keeps ids only** (`events/persistence.rs:301-321`). A test pins that the preview is never persisted: a later-forgotten note's title must not survive there (03 §6).
  - The unscoped pending read `GET /v1/chat/confirmations` does carry the preview. That widens T22 (pending) with note text under the same bearer; §10 notes it.
- **GUI.** `ToolConfirmationBanner` shows the preview above the literal arguments, labelled "From OpenAlpaca", and the arguments stay available. With `per_call` the composer drops **Always allow**. DESIGN_SPEC §3.14's "literal arguments, never summarised" rule gains the exception: *a daemon-authored preview, never the model's text* — since rev 3.41, *composed from the notes, never from the call's arguments*: since LQ11 the suggested text can be the chat model's own, and the card says so.
- **CLI.** The inline prompt and `tasks confirmations list|watch` print the preview before the JSON arguments.

### 4.5 How the assistant learns there is a suggestion

On main-loop turns where `memory_confirm` is offered, the per-turn memory section appends a `<memory_suggestions>` block. It is built with the recall, outside the cached layer (03 §5), and delivered as quoted data under the trust label:
- It holds at most **3** pending suggestions, newest first, one line each, in at most 1 KiB, and that counts against the 8 KiB memory budget.
- Each line reads `[memory-suggestion id=<B> revision=<rB> replaces=<X> replaces_revision=<rX>] “<X title>” (saved by you) → “<B title>” (from what you said on <date>)`, with the date taken as in §4.4 — "(suggested by the assistant on <date>)" for one the chat model wrote (rev 3.34, §4.2 rule 5).
- Only suggestions that `memory_confirm` would accept are listed: origin `extracted_user` and a user-authored cited row (§4.2 rule 5). An inferred suggestion never appears.
- A suggestion already **raised on this lane** since the daemon started is left out. The queue keeps that set in memory, and `memory_confirm`'s preview hook adds to it. A timed-out or denied card is not re-raised in the same conversation.
- The block carries no instructions. When to call is in `memory_confirm`'s description (§4.1), because instructions inside quoted data are ignored by design.

It is read by a new `MemoryService::pending_suggestions(ctx, limit)`, which reads with the turn's `ModelTool` read context. It is not a recall, so it adds nothing to `memory_used`.

**Its cost is on the reply path, so it reads the index, not the partition.** Candidates and `supersedes` are reachable today only through `Partition::load()` (`memory/service/mod.rs:751-757`, `:821-825`), while recall uses the index plus verification (`memory/service/recall.rs:172-220`). A parse on every attended GUI turn would grow with the note count. `pending_suggestions` therefore selects `effective_status = 'candidate' AND supersedes <> '[]' AND origin = 'extracted_user'` from the index's new `supersedes` column (LR18, §2.3), newest first. It then re-reads files until three qualify, reading at most ten. The service bench measures it (§6.4).

---

## 5. Search for every agent (`memory_read`)

Six shipped templates grant `memory_read`: `lead_agent`, `general_agent`, `code_agent`, `planning_agent`, `review_agent` and `explore_agent` (`config/agents/lead_agent.md:8` and so on). **Three do not:** `writing_agent`, `research_agent` and `system_agent` (`config/agents/research_agent.md:5-9`). None denies it. The design is written for the recommended answer to LQ3:

- **Shipped templates.** The three missing templates list `memory_read`, so all nine name it.
- **An ambient grant covers the owner's install without a re-seed.** The owner's `config/agents/` is never re-seeded (`apps/openalpacad/src/bootstrap/content.rs:102-108`), and a factory reset does not touch it. So `AgentTemplate::to_subagent` appends `memory_read` to `capabilities` and `allowed_capabilities` for every **internal** template (`AgentSource::Internal`, `agent/template/mod.rs:21-28`, `:92`) that neither lists it nor names it in `denied_capabilities`.
  - This is exactly the `workspace_read`/`workspace_write` precedent (`crates/openalpaca_core/src/agent/template/mod.rs:501-521`).
  - `denied_capabilities` still wins (`tools/registry/mod.rs:894-900`).
  - **A plugin-contributed template (`AgentSource::Plugin`) gets `memory_read` only when it names it.** A plugin-backed subagent runs as the run's owner (`runner/lead_agent/tools.rs:343`). Its proxied tool calls are checked against the same widened policy (`tools.rs:575-581`), and `run_plugin_agent_loop` sends each result back to the plugin's process (`runner/plugin_agent.rs:241-247`). An ambient grant would therefore hand every note the owner has to third-party code by default. The memory read tools never prompt (`tools/builtins/mod.rs:588-590`), so no card would stand in the way. A plugin template that names `memory_read` keeps getting it, exactly as today.
- **Ownership is unchanged.** A subagent's reads use the run's owner (`runner/lead_agent/tools.rs:338-358`), and a `system` run refuses (03 §3).
- **Exposure.** Notes arrive as trust-labelled quoted data. `research_agent` also has `web_access`, as `general_agent` and `planning_agent` already do.
- **Cost.** Three read-only tool definitions per prompt, which never prompt (`builtins/mod.rs:588-590`). They count against small budgets such as research's 12 tool calls and 8 rounds (`research_agent.md:19-22`), but a call is spent only when the agent chooses to search.
- **What 03 must say.** §7's "**no agent template changes**" (line 359) is revised (§11).

---

## 6. Smaller items

### 6.1 Validity dates in the recall header

`RecalledNote` and `VerifiedItem` gain `valid_from`, `valid_to` and `timing: Option<Timing::Upcoming>`. The bounds are filled from the re-read `Note` at both construction sites (`memory/service/recall.rs:254-272`, `:363-413`, `:565-581`); `timing` comes from the index (below). `render` (`types.rs:385-417`) appends ` valid_from=<ts|-> valid_to=<ts|->` **only when a bound is set**, followed by ` timing=upcoming` when the index says the window has not begun:

```text
[memory id=… revision=… path=notes/…md kind=episode scope=global heading="" lines=1-6 match=title freshness=verified sources=- reason=recall valid_from=2026-10-05T00:00:00Z valid_to=2026-10-20T00:00:00Z timing=upcoming]
```

- **Unwindowed notes.** They render byte-for-byte as today, so no existing expectation moves (`memory/service/tests.rs:1766-1781`, `prompt_ctx/sources/memory.rs:364`).
- **Ended windows.** These never reach automatic recall (`memory_index/search.rs:186-228`, `memory_index/mod.rs:729-757`), so `upcoming` is the only non-current value the header carries.
- **Clock.** "Upcoming" must use the index's clock, so the header and the filter agree. `MemoryIndex::now` is private to the storage crate (`memory_index/mod.rs:527-529`), and the header is built in core, so no visibility change could reach it. Instead each `SearchHit` and pinned row the index returns gains `upcoming: bool`, computed in the same query as the validity filter (`valid_from > instant`, `memory_index/search.rs:186-228`) and on the same clock. No new clock API is needed.
- **Trust label.** `TRUST_LABEL` (`recall.rs:21`) gains: "`valid_from`/`valid_to` are a note's effective window (`valid_to` exclusive); `timing=upcoming` means the window has not begun, so the note describes a plan, not the present."
- **Search results.** For parity, `memory_search` results gain `valid_from` and `valid_to` (RFC 3339 or null; §11).
- **What is not shown.** `observed_at` and the title are not added to the header. The owner asked for validity. `observed_at` is now set for learned notes (§2.5), so a later revision can add it.

### 6.2 A subagent's notes in *recently used*

- **How a subagent reaches memory.** Only through its own `memory_search` and `memory_read` calls, and those already reach the ring with its `task_id`. `ToolContext.task_id` is set for every subagent (`runner/lead_agent/tools.rs:342`), `context_for_tool` copies it into the `Attribution` (`memory/service/mod.rs:302-325`), and a `ModelTool` search records what it returned (`recall.rs:530-543`; `read` records at `memory/service/mod.rs:700`). §5 gives every internal template those tools, which is what makes a subagent's notes reach *recently used*.
- **The other path carries nothing.** A distilled package section would carry memory into a subagent's prompt, and it would not be recorded: `distill` drops the section key (`prompt_ctx/manager.rs:149-154`) and `to_bundle` re-keys it `Package` (`prompt_ctx/package.rs:120`). But the lead's parent bundle is `ContextBundle::empty()` (`runner/lead_agent/mod.rs:229-231`), and the spawn path says so (`tools.rs:427-430`). No production run puts memory in a package today.
- **So nothing is edited (ruling LR15).** One test pins the owner's ask on the real path: a subagent's `memory_search` produces a ring row carrying its `task_id`. The package path is recorded as residue: whoever first fills the lead's parent bundle with memory must key the section and record its delivery then. Rev 1's three edits (`PackageSection.memory_used`, the `to_bundle` key, `SpawnSubagentTool::with_memory_service`) would have changed no production behaviour, and they are dropped.

### 6.3 Parsed partitions: five quick wins, then a cache

**Quick wins.** Each is independently testable and changes no output:
1. `Partition::reconcile`'s "gone" check uses `HashSet`s and becomes O(N), where today it is O(N²) (`partition.rs:1358-1363`).
2. `forget` does one `load_with` per call, not one per target (`partition.rs:1140-1141`).
3. `commit_write` returns the post-write view, so `refresh_navigation`, the service's `reindex` and `update`'s origin read-back reuse it (`partition.rs:887-950`, `:1384-1389`; `memory/service/mutations.rs:79-87`). Full parses per call go from 3 to 1 for `store` and from 5–6 to 2 for `update`.
4. `reindex(full = false)` builds `IndexRecord`s, and clones bodies, only for notes whose fingerprint changed (`memory/service/mod.rs:462-515`).
5. Existing `fact_key`s come from the index, not from a partition parse (§2.3).

**The cache.**
- **Shape.** `memory_files::NoteParseCache` is owned by the service beside `fingerprints` and `last_scans` (`memory/service/mod.rs:155`, `:160`). It is handed to `Partition::open_*` and consulted in `parse_all` (`partition.rs:682-734`).
  - **Key:** `(partition_key, file_name)`.
  - **Valid while** the file's `(mtime_ns, size, inode, ctime)` are unchanged. These are the stat identity the reconciliation scan uses (`memory/service/mod.rs:1214-1224`), plus `ctime`, which no user tool can set back.
  - **Value:** `Arc<(Note, revision)>`, the full parsed note, so `Note`, `NoteView` and every consumer stay as they are (`memory_files/note.rs:217-238`).
- **Bound.** The cache holds at most **128 MiB**, the constant `NOTE_PARSE_CACHE_BYTES`, accounted as body bytes plus metadata. It works all-or-nothing per partition, with least-recently-used eviction across partitions. A partition that would not fit is parsed uncached, as today. Per-file LRU is avoided because a full load scans every file, which thrashes it. 128 MiB holds about 70K average notes (1.3 KB body) or about 180K learned-size notes (§6.4 measures both).
- **Snapshot.** The parsed `ControlSnapshot` is cached, keyed by its file stat plus an in-process generation that every `save` bumps (`snapshot.rs:250-258`).
- **Invalidation.**
  - **Service writes** update the cache directly in `commit_write`: lstat the renamed file and insert. A retitle removes the old file name's entry (`partition.rs:916-929`), and `forget` removes entries.
  - **External edits** show up as a stat mismatch, so that one file is re-read. This is 03 §5's "changed files are re-hashed".
  - **A workspace move or an index rebuild** evicts the entries wherever `fingerprints` and `last_scans` are evicted (`memory/service/mod.rs:401-412`, `:623-626`).
  - `doctor --repair` and `adopt` run with the daemon stopped, so no cache can be stale across them.
- **The invariant.** **A mutation re-reads and re-hashes the file it changes before its revision check** (`partition.rs:970-984`, `:1050-1057`, `:1141-1156`). Otherwise a same-size, same-mtime external edit would match a cached revision and be overwritten.
- **Testing the invariant.** A real edit cannot reproduce a same-stat change: writing a file and then setting its mtime back (`File::set_modified`) both move `ctime`, so the cache misses on `ctime` and the test would pass with the invariant reverted. The cache therefore takes its stat function as a parameter, `NoteParseCache::with_stat(fn(&Path) -> StatKey)`, defaulting to the real `lstat`. The test injects one that reports the pre-edit key after an external edit. It then shows that a mutation still re-hashes and refuses, and that the same test fails once the re-hash is removed.

### 6.4 The 100K bench and the index size: measure first

- **Bench.** `memory_index/bench.rs` gains `run_bench(n, mix)`, shared by today's 10K test and a new `#[ignore]`d `bench_search_100k_notes_with_verification`. Both run in release: `cargo test --release -p openalpaca_storage -- --ignored bench_ --nocapture`, the same as the existing test (`bench.rs:1-3`, `:27`, `:336-443`).
- **What each run prints:**
  - build time;
  - cache MiB, with the **`dbstat`** breakdown (`SELECT name, SUM(pgsize) FROM dbstat GROUP BY name`, compiled into the bundled SQLite);
  - search + verification p50/p95/max;
  - the empty-query rate;
  - the machine: `available_parallelism`, `std::env::consts` and the hostname via `Command`, as `memory_eval.rs:1312-1330` does.
- **Mixes.** A second mix, `learned`, has 70 % of notes at 200–400 B, the shape of §2.6's bodies.
- **RSS** is measured outside the process, as `/usr/bin/time -l target/release/deps/openalpaca_storage-<hash> --ignored bench_search_100k --nocapture`, so the compile is not counted.
- **Service-level bench.** A new `#[ignore]`d `memory/service/bench.rs` in core seeds by writing files and building the snapshot in one pass, through `repair`'s path. It does not seed through `create`, which would make seeding quadratic.
  - Sizes: 1K, 10K and 100K notes.
  - What it measures:
    - `open_default` with a valid cache and with a missing one (the boot path, `apps/openalpacad/src/services/mod.rs:218-221`);
    - recall p50/p95, including `touch`'s stat scan;
    - `store`, `update`, `forget`, `list` and `recently_learned` latency;
    - `learning_view` for one chunk and `pending_suggestions`, the one learning read on the reply path (§4.5);
    - the first reconciliation tick;
    - snapshot and `MEMORY.md` sizes.
  - Each is measured with the parse cache on and with it off (`cache_bytes = 0`).
- **Proposed targets, not gates:** recall p95 ≤ 100 ms and learned-`store` p95 ≤ 100 ms at 100K notes on the M5 Max. 04 §5's 50 ms p95 target stays the 10K gate.

**The index layout is not changed in this wave (ruling LR16).** *(Rev 3.36: the rule below fired in L8, the owner took the revision (LQ17), and §6.5 is it, measured on a prototype. The fixture costs below were the first estimate; §6.5.6 has the measured ones — 23's `excerpt_contains` is step 12, a `read`, which the index runner skips.)* The survey's replica of the index schema reproduced the reported 10K size within 2 % (105.4 MiB against 107.7 MiB). It projects today's layout at **1,020 MiB (8.2×) at 100K**. The trigram index is 48 % of that, and the `chunk` table, which holds the text twice plus title, aliases and tags on every chunk, is 33 %.
- **The one candidate:** trigram only over title, aliases, tags and CJK-bearing chunks, with a `unicode61` prefix fallback; both FTS tables contentless; one copy of the chunk text. The replica gives about 352 MiB (2.8×).
- **Why it waits.** It reverses 03 §5 step 4 and §7c Q9, which keep the trigram companion on every term of three or more characters (G23), so it is a design revision.
- **Its cost on the fixtures:**
  - **21:** step 1 would pass only through the title ("Editor preference") plus the prefix fallback, which would have to be reported as `trigram` unless a `prefix` `MatchReason` is added (§7a is frozen). The fixture's intent, `prefer` inside the body's `prefers`, would then rest on the prefix query, and a body-only variant would be added.
  - **21 and 23:** the index-level `excerpt_contains` checks (`crates/openalpaca_storage/src/memory_index/fixture_tests.rs:568-573`) would need the index runner to read excerpts from the file, as the service already does (`recall.rs:386-393`).
  - **23:** steps 5–6 (`简洁的回答`, `用户喜欢`) keep working, because CJK chunks keep trigram.
  - **22 and 24:** unaffected.
- **Why `detail=none/column` is out.** It breaks every phrase of four or more characters with "phrase queries are not supported (detail!=full)".
- **The decision rule, run in L8:**
  - If the 100K run shows search + verification p95 above 100 ms, the layout revision is written with its measured Rust numbers and taken to the owner.
  - If only the size is large, the owner is shown the size table and the projected alternatives, and decides whether about 1 GiB of disposable cache at 100K notes needs the revision.

### 6.5 The index layout, revised (LQ17)

> **Owner, 2026-09-26 (LQ17):** "Write the layout revision." §6.4's rule fired in L8. At 100K notes of the average mix, index search + verification is p95 150 ms and the cache 1,065 MiB, of which `fts_tri_data` is 534 MiB and `chunk` 354 MiB. The profile puts 57 % of search in the trigram leg and 42 % of FTS time in `pread`, because the file is four times the 256 MiB mmap window (the session scratchpad's `memeval/l8/bench/scale/analysis.md` and its sceptic review).

This section is the revision. It does five things:
- it narrows the trigram index to titles, aliases, tags and the text of scripts `unicode61` cannot split;
- it queries the word table for the beginnings of words;
- it lets the word table see a script boundary as a word boundary;
- it keeps one copy of the text;
- it adds `prefix` to `MatchReason`.

It amends 03 §5 step 4, §7a's `MatchReason` and §7c Q9 and Q12 (03 revs 7.7 and 7.8); the build's two repair rounds amend §5 step 4, Q9 and Q12 again (03 revs 7.10 and 7.11). Nothing outside the disposable cache changes shape: no migration, no route, and no wire field beyond the one enum value.

*Rev 3.39 is the build's repair round 1, after a review of the build (revision log): the whole-word queries at the cap search bodies too (§6.5.2), the unsegmented scripts are widened to those whose words `unicode61` breaks at their vowel signs, index schema 5 (§6.5.1), a query word glued across scripts gets the evidence the word table gives it (§6.5.3), and §6.5.4 names letters and digits run together. §6.5.5's scale, learning, coverage and hit-set figures are the build's own now.*

*Rev 3.40 is the build's repair round 2, after a second review of the build (revision log): a literal's parts are searched unless the literal itself is found, and a longer form counts as the literal only when it holds it verbatim (§6.5.2); a literal ending in a number is a prefix whatever the number's length (§6.5.2); §6.5.4 names the one whole-word loss at the cap, and draws the line of what is lost by what `unicode61` splits into words, not by spaces; §6.5.5 states the latency rule for the query shapes it holds for, with the whole message the recall sends, the service bench and its open memory finding. No index schema change: both code changes are on the query side.*

*Rev 3.41 is the close-out (revision log): the lead accepted the one row the proxy loses (tq-0032, §6.5.5); four behaviours no test pinned are pinned (§6.5.2, §6.5.3); the header table's relevance is kept, and §6.5.3 and §6.5.5 state its cost — its BM25 is normalized by the header's length alone, so it favours a short title — measured against five length-free variants, each a net loss on the proxy; the cliff at the cap is named as it fires, on body length alone (§6.5.4); O5 and `prefix=` stay open against the service's unmet recall target (§6.5.5, §6.5.8); and the Devanagari skeleton residue is recorded (§6.5.3). The citations of "today's" code in §6.5.1–§6.5.3 are to schema 3 at `f6f40feb`, the prototype's base; at `13b7a3f9` `parse_query` is `query.rs:414`, `ChunkText` `search.rs:846`, the `ON DELETE CASCADE`s `memory_index/mod.rs:460-473`, `PRAGMA foreign_keys` `:1656` and the mmap window `MMAP_BYTES` `:1650`.*

*Rev 3.37 answers a review of rev 3.36, and the revision log takes the findings one by one. What changed:*
- *A Latin term no longer matches inside a Latin word of a Chinese body (§6.5.2).*
- *When the word table's query fills its cap, the terms it sent as prefixes are searched again as whole words in titles, aliases and tags (§6.5.2).*
- *A term carrying an unsegmented character, or a stop word, is never a prefix.*
- *A trigger keeps the FTS rows in step with `chunk` (§6.5.1).*
- *The measured case now includes 30,000 distractors, a dense bench mix, the learning lookup and L8's coverage search (§6.5.5).*
- *One of the eight proxy totals loses a row. Rev 3.36 counted that as no loss; it is now stated (§6.5.5) for the lead to rule on.*

**Where the numbers come from.** A prototype of this layout was built in a scratch copy of `f6f40feb`, outside the repository, and measured against the same commit on the same machine (the M5 Max). Its final form is the variant the notes call E6; rev 3.36 measured E2. Five measures were used:
- the index benches of §6.4, with a 30K run and a *dense* mix added;
- the **retrieval proxy**, the evaluation harness's dry run (`scripts/eval/memory/README.md`, *A dry run with no model*): system D with no model, no judge and outbound networking denied, which measures what the per-turn recall alone delivers, over the 470 held-out and 150 tuning questions in both recall modes;
- the **scale proxy**: the same 620 questions searched at the index level, as the per-turn recall searches them (automatic mode, its 20 candidates), over the 83 gold notes as the proxy seeds them. The notes are searched alone, then pooled in one partition with 30,000 notes of the bench's average mix, then with those and the 319 notes two L8 runs learned (`store-L`, `store-L-episodes`). Each relied-on note's rank is read off the whole ranking;
- the **learning lookup** (`learning_view`'s search), without a model;
- **L8's coverage search**, without a model.

The build, phase 2 of the LQ17 slice, measures everything again on the real code. The notes, the reports, the harness and the prototype are in the session scratchpad (`memeval/l8/layout/`: `design.md`, `proto/`, `scale/`, `work/`).

#### 6.5.1 What each table holds (index schema 4; 5 since repair round 1)

| Table | Holds | Change from schema 3 |
|---|---|---|
| `note` | the catalog row, plus `norm_aliases` and `norm_tags` (the normalized aliases and tags, one per line) | the header text is kept once per note, for the ranker and the short-term pass |
| `chunk` | id, note, partition, ordinal, heading, line span, and `body`: the chunk's text, normalized (`query::normalize`) | `excerpt` (the text as written) and the per-chunk `title`, `aliases` and `tags` go; `body` is the one copy of the text in the cache |
| `fts_word` | `unicode61 remove_diacritics 2` over title, aliases, tags and body, for every chunk, each split at script boundaries | contentless; the text is split |
| `fts_tri_head` (new) | `trigram` over title, aliases and tags, for every chunk | the header's trigram index |
| `fts_tri` | `trigram` over the body, only for a chunk whose text carries a character of an **unsegmented script** | was every chunk's four columns |

- **Unsegmented scripts.** These are the scripts `unicode61` cannot split into words. They are of two kinds *(rev 3.39, index schema 5; rev 3.37 named only the first five of the first kind)*:
  - **Written without spaces between words:** CJK, as `query::is_cjk` defines it, plus Thai, Lao, Myanmar, Khmer, Tibetan, Yi, Javanese, Balinese, Buginese and the Tai scripts. `unicode61` reads a run of any of them as one token, so a word inside the run is found only as a substring.
  - **Built with vowel signs `unicode61` breaks a word at:** Devanagari and the other Brahmic scripts (U+0900–U+0DFF, their extensions and the Brahmic blocks of plane 1), Thaana, the Philippine scripts, Limbu, Lepcha, Sundanese, Batak, Cham, Meetei Mayek and Miao. `unicode61`'s tables count only letters, numbers and private-use characters as token characters, so it breaks a word at every combining vowel sign and virama: `दोस्तों` is indexed as `द स त`, `சென்னை` as `ச ன ன`, and `बात` and `बीत` are the same fragments. No query or ranker reads those fragments as the word — `query::tokens` keeps the vowel signs — so this text needs the trigram table as much as Chinese does. Rev 3.37's claim that the word table serves "the other space-separated scripts" did not hold for them (the review of the build, finding M2).
  - Latin, Cyrillic, Greek, Arabic, Hebrew and the other scripts whose words `unicode61` reads whole are split into words, and the word table serves them. The vowel points of Hebrew, Arabic, Syriac and Samaritan are marks `unicode61` breaks at too, but ordinary text in those scripts is unpointed, so they are left out. A test (`every_character_unicode61_breaks_a_word_at_is_unsegmented_or_listed`) indexes every character `query::tokens` keeps inside a word in the bundled SQLite and fails on any that `unicode61` breaks a word at and `is_unsegmented` does not cover, but for that listed set — so a narrowed range, or a SQLite with other tables, is caught.
  - `query::is_unsegmented` is the one predicate. The upsert, the query builder, the ranker and the file scan all call it, and it decides what `fts_tri` and the split hold. A change to its ranges is therefore a change to the index's contents (§6.5.7).
- **Script boundaries.** `unicode61` reads a run of letters and digits as one token whatever its scripts, so in `和commit` the word `commit` is not a token at all.
  - Where an unsegmented character meets a letter or digit of another script, `query::split_scripts` puts a space. `fts_word` indexes `和 commit`, and the word table's phrases are split the same way, as `normalize` already is on both sides.
  - The ranker and the file scan read the same tokens (`query::tokens_split`, slices of the text cut at those boundaries).
  - A CJK run stays one token; `2026年7月` becomes `2026 年 7 月`.
  - This is not rare. In the L8 stores, 7 of the 114 notes carrying Chinese have Latin words glued to it (29 occurrences: `在GUI`, `和commit`, `用bullet`).
  - `fts_tri` holds the text unsplit, so a substring across the boundary (`在gui`) is still found there.
- **Contentless.** All three FTS tables are `content='', contentless_delete=1` (SQLite 3.43 or later; the bundled library is 3.51.1).
  - Today both tables are external-content over `chunk`, with insert and delete triggers (`memory_index/mod.rs:463-484`). Deleting a row needs the four text columns in `chunk`.
  - Instead, the upsert writes each chunk's FTS rows itself — two, or three for an unsegmented chunk — using the chunk's id as the rowid.
  - One `AFTER DELETE ON chunk` trigger removes them: `DELETE FROM fts_word WHERE rowid = old.id`, and the same for the other two tables. `contentless_delete=1` allows it; this was verified on SQLite 3.51, through a cascade too.
  - So every way a chunk row goes takes its FTS rows with it: the explicit deletes, and a cascade from `note` alike (`chunk.note_rowid … ON DELETE CASCADE` under `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON`, `mod.rs:449`, `:1599`).
  - That matters because chunk ids are reused (`INTEGER PRIMARY KEY` without `AUTOINCREMENT`): an orphaned FTS row would attach to the next chunk that takes its id.
  - No path relies on the cascade today, since every delete removes the chunk rows first (`mod.rs:1544-1585`). The trigger keeps it that way by construction. Rev 3.36 deleted the FTS rows by hand before each of those deletes.
  - BM25 and the `rank` configuration work on contentless tables. `highlight()` and `snippet()` would not, and nothing calls them.
- **The one copy is the normalized text.** The ranker compares every candidate chunk's text with the query terms (`ChunkText`, `memory_index/search.rs:726`). The one- and two-character pass scans up to 4,096 chunks' text (`search.rs:514`). Both work on normalized text.
  - Keeping the text as written instead would mean normalizing every candidate chunk per query (up to 256 per FTS query) and up to 4,096 in the short-term pass.
  - Keeping no text would move ranking after verification. The index would then read files (§7c Q11): one per candidate note, hundreds per search, not only the ≤ `limit` notes the service verifies now.
  - The text as written belongs to the file. The service already takes an excerpt from the re-read file at the hit's chunk ordinal and never reads the index's copy (`memory/service/recall.rs:576`). So `SearchHit.excerpt` (`search.rs:173`) goes, and nothing on the request path changes.
- **Two trigram tables, not one.** FTS5's bm25 normalizes by a row's total length.
  - With the header and the unsegmented body in one row, a Latin term's title hit on a Chinese note ranked below the same hit on an English note, whose row was now its title alone. Chinese notes sank in ties on a shared English word (hq-0289, hq-0295 and tq-0073 on the prototype, all "Lantern …" notes with Chinese bodies).
  - The prototype measured it: with one table the two excerpts runs of the proxy lost 2–3 and 2 rows; with two they gained 2 and 1 (§6.5.5).
  - The header table repeats the header on each chunk, as today. Its rowids are then chunk ids, and every leg of a search returns the same kind of candidate.

#### 6.5.2 Term routing (03 §7c Q9, revised)

The query parser is unchanged (`query.rs:174`): words, literals and short terms, stop words and `MAX_TERMS` 32 all stay as they are. Each term goes to the tables as follows:

| Term | `fts_word` | `fts_tri_head` | `fts_tri` (unsegmented bodies) |
|---|---|---|---|
| a word of ≥ 3 characters | as a **prefix query**, `"w"*`: the whole token and every token it begins. A word that carries an unsegmented character, or is a stop word, goes exactly, `"w"` | as a phrase (a substring) | only a word that carries an unsegmented character, as a phrase, ranked |
| a literal (an identifier or a path) | as a phrase; its last token is a prefix under the word's rule, when that token has ≥ 3 characters or *(rev 3.40)* is a number | as a phrase | as for a word |
| a one- or two-character term | unchanged: the bounded substring pass over the note's normalized title, aliases and tags and each chunk's body | ← | ← |

Every phrase sent to `fts_word` is split at script boundaries first, as its text was (§6.5.1).

- **What `prefer` finds.**
  - `"prefer"*` matches `prefer`, `prefers`, `preferred` and `preference`, whether in a body, a title, an alias or a tag.
  - `config` finds `configure` and `configuration`, and no longer finds `reconfigure`.
  - `memory_file` (a literal) is the phrase `memory` `file*`, so it still finds `memory_files`.
  - `partner's` stays the exact phrase `partner` `s`, because its last token is one character. As a prefix it matched `partner store` in another note, so the literal counted as found and its part `partner` was never searched; the prototype lost hq-0009 ("The user's partner Tomas") to that. *(Rev 3.40: a longer form now counts as the literal found only where it holds the literal verbatim, next bullet, so that loss cannot recur that way; `partner's` stays exact all the same, since `partner` `s*` would give every note that says `partner store` a `phrase`.)*
  - `and`, in a query of nothing but stop words (kept then, §7c Q9), matches `and` and not `android`.
  - `commit` finds `和commit` as a whole word (`term`), and `用户喜欢` finds `用户喜欢简洁的回答` as a substring (`trigram`).
- **When a literal counts as found** *(rev 3.40; the second review of the build, finding M1)*. A literal's parts are searched only when the literal itself is found in no eligible note (§7c Q9, unchanged).
  - Schema 3 decided *found* by the literal's exact phrase on the word table, or a verbatim substring on its trigram table. Revs 3.36–3.39 decided it with the query the search sends, the prefix phrase, so any longer form counted, whatever its separator: `dark modes`, `code reviews`, `time zones` and `side projects` hid the parts of `dark-mode`, `code-review`, `time-zone` and `side-project`. A note holding one part whole — "a dark theme at night", "a careful review", "the user's current project" — was then not a candidate at all, in a store of any size. The design had exempted only a one- or two-character last token (`partner's`, above).
  - Now a literal is found by its exact phrase, by a substring either trigram table holds, or by a longer form the prefix phrase finds **that holds the literal verbatim** in its body: `memory_file` in `memory_files` (fixture 26, step 9), not `dark-mode` in `dark modes`. The prefix phrase still finds candidates and gives `phrase` evidence. A header needs no verbatim check: a literal has three or more characters, so the header trigram table finds it there.
  - On the review's 19-note probe every such note is back at schema 3's rank: 2nd or 3rd, `term`; `time-zone`'s note is 2nd as `prefix` (`time*` in `timezone`), where schema 3 said `trigram`. Over the 620 evaluation questions at the index level, one hit list changes — tq-0117, whose relied-on note stays 1st, gets schema 3's second hit back — and the relied-on notes within 2, 8, 10 and 20 stay 447, 509, 514 and 541.
  - As in schema 3, a note holding the phrase with another separator (`dark mode` for `dark-mode`) is the literal found, and hides its parts.
  - The file scan decides the same way (`ChunkText::finds_literal`); `a_literal_found_only_in_a_longer_form_still_routes_its_parts` pins both.
- **A number ends a prefix** *(rev 3.40; the second review, finding m1)*. A literal whose last token is a number is a prefix whatever the number's length.
  - `10:30` is `10` `30*` and finds `10:30am`, which `unicode61` reads as `10`, `30am`; `v1.2` finds `v1.2beta`, and `3.12` finds `3.12rc1`. Rev 3.39 found none of them, and found `2026-09-15T10:00Z` for `2026-09-15` only through the part `2026`; it is a `phrase` now.
  - The three-character floor was set for letters: `partner's` must not find `partner store`. A digit continuing a number is not that case.
  - The cost is the merge of every token that begins with the number. Measured search-only, median of 5, at 100K notes of the learned mix, whose evidence lines date nearly every note (`2026-09-…`): `v1.2` 0.04 → 5.3 ms; `2026.2` 32 → 45 ms; `09.2` 0.05 → 38 ms, because it now finds the thousands of `09-2…` dates it begins. `2026-09-1` falls from 31 to 22 ms, a phrase where rev 3.39 searched the part `2026` in every note. Nothing moves at 30K of the average mix, which has no dates.
  - A number inside a word is still not found (`1.2` in `v1.2beta`, §6.5.4). `a_literal_ending_in_a_number_finds_the_text_it_begins` pins it.
- **The whole-word queries at the cap.** When the word table's query fills its candidate cap (256) and some term went to it as a prefix, those terms are searched again as whole words *(rev 3.39: in bodies too)*:
  - all of them together in titles, aliases and tags (`{title aliases tags} : (…)`), ranked, with a cap of their own;
  - and each one alone in bodies (`{body} : "w"`), unranked: the query reads at most 257 rows, and the chunks join the candidates only when there are at most 256 of them.

  Below the cap the first query already holds every such match, so a small store never runs them.
  - **Why.** A beginning shared by many longer words fills the cap with them. In the scale proxy at 30,000 notes, `work*` filled it: the synthetic vocabulary has `worker` and `workspace` but never `work`. That pushed out "The user's current job at Quillpay", whose one match for "Where do I work and what is my role?" is its tag `work`. "用户之前在 Norden Labs 工作" fell out the same way. The current layout kept both, through the trigram companion's own 256. Without the second query, two relied-on notes leave the pool; with it, none does (§6.5.5).
  - **Bodies are crowded out too, and first** (rev 3.39; the review of the build, finding M1). Rev 3.37 reasoned that the notes a prefix crowds out are those whose match is short and strong — a title, an alias, a tag — and searched the header only. But the word table's BM25 weighs a body match below a tag (1.0 against 1.5), so a note holding the word only in its body is crowded out before the tag-`work` note. On the bench's own corpus, with short learned-style notes added whose title does not hold the word ("The user rides to work on a bicycle", "…wears an Apple watch…"), 8 of 15 questions at 30,000 notes lost the one note holding the word — `work`, "how do I get to work?", `apple`, `mark`, `extra`, `doc`, `check`, `head` — which schema 3 ranked first or second; at 10,000, `mark` and `doc` were lost already. The scale proxy could not see it: its gold notes carry 4.8 aliases each, so a header match nearly always exists.
  - **Why the header ranked, and each body term alone and unranked.** One whole-word query over every column recovers the body notes and loses the header ones: its own cap fills with body matches, so two relied-on notes of the scale proxy stay out (126 unranked against 124, §6.5.5). A ranked body query beside the header one recovers both, but computes BM25 for every chunk that holds a common word: +11 ms of p95 at 30K and +34 ms at 100K (47.7 → 82.1 ms; dense 64.6 → 84.0; §6.5.5). The body query is therefore one per term, with no BM25, reading at most 257 rows, and it adds a term's chunks only when they fit within the cap. A rare word — the case the review found — is added whole; a word held by more than 256 eligible chunks adds none, since a cap could not single out any one note holding it (schema 3's exact-word leg kept 256 of them, by BM25). The candidates are then the first query's own 256. They keep the best of those notes only when the longer words do not outrank them; when more than 256 chunks holding a longer form outrank them on BM25 — by a header column, or by a shorter row alone — they can keep none (§6.5.4, and a test pins the rule — *rev 3.40*, the second review, tests F1; rev 3.39 said the first query "keeps the best of them"; *rev 3.41*, the third review, retrieval m2: rev 3.40 named titles only, and a test pins the cap's boundary at exactly 256). It recovers every note the ranked query does, on the scale proxy and on the review's probes, within the benches' noise (§6.5.5). The header query stays as it was: short rows, few matches, ranked at under 3.4 ms.
  - **A larger cap alone does not do it.** At 512 both notes stayed out, and at 1,024 one did. A tag hit ranks too low among hundreds of `worker` bodies for any cap that bounds the work.
- **Why always a prefix, and not only when the exact token misses.** A per-term fallback depends on the corpus. In a large store almost every word occurs exactly somewhere, so the fallback would never fire where it matters, and a small test store would pass what a real one fails.
- **Why from 3 characters.** It is the same threshold as the rest of routing. With prefixes only from 4 characters the proxy was worse (index held-out 250 rows against 255), and the latencies of §6.5.5 are measured with 3. *(Rev 3.41: pinned — `tea` finds `teams` and `x_abc` finds `x_abcd`, which a threshold of 4 fails; and `Literal::word_probes`' split at script boundaries is pinned by a glued literal found spaced.)*
- **A Latin term is matched in a body only as a word or a word's beginning.** That holds in an unsegmented body as well, where the split has made `commit` a word of `和commit`.
  - Rev 3.36 searched the body trigram table for every term, and credited a Latin substring inside any chunk that carried one CJK character. The note template's headings then matched on every Chinese or mixed note and on no English one: `now` inside `knowledge` and `check` inside `recheck`, 287 question–note pairs over 25 questions of the evaluation set.
  - For hq-0090 ("…open right now?"), every note below its three real matches was a Chinese note found by that `now` alone. The current layout interleaves English and Chinese notes there.
  - For hq-0120, an English note that names 林薇 rose from 12th to 5th on `now` inside `knowledge`.
  - On the revised rule, neither list holds a note found only by the letters of a heading.
  - The split also gives a glued word the evidence it has in an English note: `term`, and the word table's BM25. Rev 3.36 gave it a substring's weight and no BM25.
  - So the body trigram table serves only terms carrying an unsegmented character, and ranks them. A Latin term's relevance comes from the word table and the header table, the same tables for every note, so a Chinese note that mentions `Lantern` in its body does not outrank an English one that says it as often. Rev 3.36's variant that ranked Latin terms in that table lost tq-0073 that way.
- **Still no stemmer.** A prefix query changes no token and no identifier: an exact word is its own prefix.
  - A term that carries an unsegmented character is never a prefix. It matches a whole run on the word table and anywhere inside a run through the trigram tables, so its reason and its score do not depend on where in the run it stands.
  - Rev 3.36 prefixed it too. `用户喜欢`, at the start of `用户喜欢简洁的回答`, then reported `prefix` with the word table's BM25, while `简洁的回答` in the same run reported `trigram` (fixture 23, steps 5 and 6).
  - A stop word is never a prefix either. It is routed only when nothing else is (§7c Q9), and then `and` would otherwise find `android`.

#### 6.5.3 Ranking and `MatchReason` (03 §7a, revised)

- **`MatchReason` gains `prefix`**: `title | alias | fact_key | term | prefix | trigram | phrase`.
  - `term` is a word matched as a whole token of the text split at script boundaries.
  - A word matched as the beginning of such a token, and nowhere as a whole token, reports `prefix` — nowhere in the note: a word held whole in one chunk drops `prefix` from another (a test pins it, rev 3.41).
  - `trigram` keeps its meaning, a substring the trigram tables found: in a title, an alias or a tag, or — for a term carrying an unsegmented character — in unsegmented text.
  - For each word, a note keeps its strongest evidence: a whole token, then a beginning, then a substring. Today a whole token already drops `trigram` (`search.rs:837`, `:928`).
- **Why a new value.** Reporting a prefix hit as `term` would claim a whole word, which it is not, and a reader weighs a whole-word match higher. Reporting it as `trigram` would name a table that did not find it.
  - Printing the new value costs nothing. Every surface maps `MatchReason::as_str`:
    - the recall header's `match=`, and the index lines' reason;
    - `memory_search`'s `match`, for the tool and the route (`memory/service/wire.rs:141`);
    - `openalpaca memory search`'s "matched on" (`commands/memory.rs:1829`);
    - `/memories` (`orchestrator/memory_commands.rs:412`).
  - `memory_used` carries `UsedReason`, not `MatchReason`.
  - The GUI types `match` as `string[]` and draws no reason (`apps/openalpaca-gui/src/lib/api/memories.ts:121`). A client that switched on the frozen list would meet a new value; none does.
- **Weights.** `W_PREFIX` is 5.0, the same as `W_TRIGRAM` (`search.rs:221`), so a prefix hit's coverage is what a substring hit's was. Its relevance is not the same:
  - A prefix hit comes from the word table's query, so it earns that table's BM25, weighted 2.0 (`W_BM25_WORD`). A substring hit earned the trigram table's, weighted 1.0.
  - The word table's column weights also favour a title (4.0) over a body (1.0).
  - That difference is tq-0032 (§6.5.5): `use*` lifts titles that begin "The user's".
  - Rev 3.36 said that a note which `prefer` found inside `prefers` "scores as it did". Its coverage does; its relevance does not.
  - Weights 3 and 8, measured on the one-table variant, did no better overall: 253 / 282 / 81 / 91 and 253 / 283 / 83 / 91, against 255 / 284 / 82 / 91 at 5 (§6.5.5's order).
  - A variant that gave a prefix hit a substring's relevance (E4) is in §6.5.8.
- **Relevance.**
  - Word relevance is the word table query's BM25, normalized over its candidates.
  - A chunk that only the whole-word queries at the cap kept takes no word relevance: the first query ranked it below its 256, and its coverage decides. Taking the header query's own BM25, normalized over its own candidates, cost 1–3 rows at each threshold of the scale proxy; the body queries compute none. A test pins it at the cap for the header and for the body: such a note scores its coverage and its authority, nothing more *(rev 3.39)*. So "how do I get to work?" at 30,000 bench notes finds the body-only `work` note again, but 30th, below 29 notes that begin both words (`get*`, `work*`) with relevance; schema 3 ranked it 2nd. At 100K it is 36th on the average mix and 28th on the learned, where schema 3 was 2nd on both (the second review, n1).
  - Trigram relevance is the larger of two normalized BM25s: the header table's, and the body table's for unsegmented terms. Today it is one table's BM25 over every body (`search.rs:217`), which counted a Latin body word twice, once in each table. The one proxy row that fell for that reason, hq-0318, is in §6.5.5.
  - **The header table's relevance favours a short header** *(rev 3.41; the third review, retrieval m1)*. `fts_tri_head`'s rows hold the header alone, so its BM25 is normalized by the header's length: among notes holding the same word in a header, the shortest header wins the header relevance, and a whole-word title match earns it on top of the word table's BM25 — the double count removed for bodies, kept for headers. It pushes down the extractor's own style of note, a descriptive title with aliases (§2.2). In the review's controlled store (2,000 background notes, 2,000 notes holding `birthday` once in a body, N titled "Birthday ⟨word⟩"), "The user's birthday" with the aliases `生日, date of birth, birthday` ranks 1st on schema 3 for "When is my birthday?" and 47th at N = 100, absent from the 50 hits at N = 300 (outside the header table's 256): 1st on the word table, last on the header table. The rank losses at scale this causes are §6.5.5's. It is kept: §6.5.5 measures five ways to take the length out of it, and each is a net loss on the proxy.
- **The file scan agrees with the index.** While the index is rebuilt, search answers from the files with the index's own evidence (`memory_index/scan.rs:140`), through the same `ChunkText`.
  - A word matches a chunk as a whole token or a word's beginning of its text split at script boundaries, or as a substring of the header. A term carrying an unsegmented character also matches as a substring of an unsegmented body.
  - A query word glued across scripts (`python版本`) matches as its split tokens in sequence — the phrase the word table is sent — and reports `term`, in spaced text (`Python 版本`) as in glued *(rev 3.39; the review's F1: the ranker compared the unsplit word, so a spaced note the index found had no reason and the file scan did not find it)*. Trigram relevance, the larger of the two tables', is pinned by a test too.
  - So an answer given during a rebuild finds what the index finds — but for one residue, pre-existing and recorded, not changed *(rev 3.41; the third review, retrieval n2)*: the word table indexes a Devanagari or other Brahmic word by its consonant skeleton (`दोस्तों` and `दोस्त` both as `द स त`, §6.5.1), and its exact phrase matches that skeleton, so `दोस्तों` returns a note holding only `दोस्त` with no reason — which the file scan, reading the words, does not return. Over the review's 1,800 random queries it is the only disagreement (13 hit sets); schema 3 returned the same hits. Keeping such terms off the word table's exact phrase is a behaviour change, for a later slice.
  - The scan reads every note, so it has no cap and needs no whole-word query.

#### 6.5.4 What is lost, measured

The layout no longer finds a term **inside** a word of a body in a script `unicode61` splits into words — Latin, Cyrillic, Greek, Arabic, Hebrew …: a suffix, an infix, or a substring across words. That now holds in a Chinese note's body too, where a Latin word is matched only whole or by its beginning. Over the 620 evaluation questions and the 83 gold notes, 928 question-term–note matches that the current layout makes are no longer made (counted by a script that mirrors the revised routing, in the scratchpad's `work/infix_loss_e3.py`):
- **722 of them (77.8 %)** are `check` inside `recheck` and `now` inside `knowledge`. These are the note template's own section headings, `## Knowledge` and `## Recheck when` — a learned note's body has the first too (§2.6) — which the trigram companion matched in nearly every note.
- **287 of the 928** are in a body that carries an unsegmented character, over 25 questions. Rev 3.36's count, 641, still made those matches. They are what the review's first finding was about (revision log, rev 3.37).
- The rest include `ever` in `never`, `port` in `support`, `call` in `locally`, `book` in `runbook` and `MacBook`, `name` in `renamed`, `test` in `nextest`, `script` in `transcript`, `file` in `justfile`, `password` in `1password` and `host` in `localhost`.
- **7** fall on a note that a question relies on, and all seven are `check` or `now`. None of them is among the proxy's lost rows: each of those notes shares a real word with its question.

**Whom it costs.** The loss falls on words written joined to other words, in a body:
- **English:** compounds (`runbook`, `nextest`, `justfile`, `1password`) and camelCase identifiers (`homeStore`).
- **German and Swedish:** there it is ordinary text, since compounds are written as one word. `wechsel` is not found in `Zeitzonenwechsel`, nor `resa` in `affärsresa`. The owner keeps notes about Stockholm.
- **Arabic and Hebrew:** articles and prepositions are written attached, so the bare word is inside the written one (`كتاب` in `الكتاب`, `בית` in `בבית`).
- **Letters and digits run together** *(rev 3.39; the review of the build, m2)*: `unicode61` reads `v0.4.2`'s `v0`, `python3.12`, `RTX4090`, `WH-1000XM5`, `sha256sum`, `2560x1440` and a commit hash as tokens that begin with their first character, so the number or the tail alone — `0.4.2`, `3.12`, `4090`, `xm5`, `256`, `1440`, `c1122` — is inside a word and is no longer found in a body, where schema 3 found each. A developer's notes are full of them — the gold notes hold 18 (`1000XM5`, `x86_64`, `arm64`, `Jinja2`, `p95`, `v1` …), and the XM5 note survives only through its alias. The whole token (`v0.4.2`) and its beginning are still found: a word's beginning from three characters (`v2026`, `457c`, `rtx`), and an identifier's when its last token has three or more characters (`memory_file` in `memory_files`) or *(rev 3.40)* is a number (`10:30` in `10:30am`, `v1.2` in `v1.2beta`, `3.12` in `3.12rc1`). An identifier ending in one or two letters stays exact (`x_ab` does not find `x_abc`), and a number inside a word is not found (`1.2` in `v1.2beta`). A note that writes the number apart (`Python 3.12`) or carries it as an alias is found by it.

A word's *beginning* is still found in all of these, `Zeitzone` in `Zeitzonenwechsel` among them. Rev 3.36 described the loss as English compounds and camelCase only.

In a title, an alias or a tag, all of them are still found. Aliases are the remedy the design already names for a wording a note does not contain (MD-1, 03 §5), and the extractor proposes them, in the other language too (§2.2). Text in an unsegmented script (§6.5.1) — Chinese, Japanese, Korean, Thai, Devanagari, Tamil and the rest — keeps substring search.

**One whole-word loss, at the cap** *(rev 3.40; the second review of the build, tests F1)*. A word held whole in more than 256 eligible bodies reaches the candidates only through the prefix query's own 256 (§6.5.2), which can hold none of those notes whenever more than 256 chunks holding a longer form outrank them on BM25 — by a header column, as in the first probe below, or by a shorter row alone, as in the second *(rev 3.41; the third review, retrieval m2: rev 3.40 said the longer words must sit in headers)*.
- The review's probe: 300 notes titled "Worker rota N" (`worker`, `workspace`, `workers`) and N notes that each say `work` once in a body, then "where do I work?". With up to 256 notes holding `work` whole, every one is added and the top 8 are all `term` hits; with 257 or more, the 50 hits are all `prefix` hits on "Worker rota" notes and none holds `work`. Schema 3 ranked the target 1st at every count the review tried, up to 600.
- The third review's probe with **no header** holding either word *(rev 3.41)*: K notes hold `car` or `log` whole in a body, 1,500 hold a longer form (`cards`, `career`, `login`, `logging` …) in a body, and 2,000 are background; the questions are "car", "log", "my car" and "daily log". With both kinds of body 8–30 words long, every one of the 50 hits holds the word whole, at K = 300 and at 600. With the whole holders' bodies 12–30 words and the longer forms' 4–20 (or 25–45 against 2–6), at K = 300 none of the 50 does: every hit is `prefix`. Every row has tf 1 and one IDF, so row length alone picks the first query's 256, and a mild skew toward shorter longer-form holders fills it — learned notes (200–300 bytes) holding `login` or `career` beside longer notes holding `log` or `car` are that skew.
- A question whose note holds two of its words ("how do I get to work?") is not lost at any N: it begins both prefixes and makes the first query's 256.
- No bench query shows it against schema 3, nor does either proxy: the gold notes are far from 256 holders of a word, and in the bench's corpus the longer forms do not outrank the whole ones. *(Rev 3.41 corrects rev 3.40's "the bench's vocabulary seldom puts the longer words in titles", which is not what keeps them clear.)* `a_body_word_held_by_more_than_the_cap_adds_none_at_the_cap` pins the rule, and `a_body_word_held_by_exactly_the_cap_is_added_at_the_cap` its boundary.
- The ranked body query of §6.5.8 would close it, at +34 ms of p95 at 100K. It is a measured trade-off, for the lead. An option the review named and nobody measured: when the per-term body query reads more than 256 rows, add its first 256 anyway — arbitrary holders by chunk id, with `term` coverage, ties by `updated_at` — rather than none.

#### 6.5.5 The measured case

**The retrieval proxy.** Per-turn recall alone: system D, no model, networking denied. Each cell counts the rows in which every relied-on note was delivered and, in index mode, the rows in which each was listed or delivered.

| Run | Current layout | Revised (prototype) | Relied-on notes delivered / listed |
|---|---|---|---|
| index, held-out (470) | 254 / 293 | **257 / 293** | 305 / 47 → 308 / 44 |
| excerpts, held-out (470) | 286 | **288** | 344 → 345 |
| index, tuning (150) | 82 / 95 | 82 / **94** | 97 / 17 → 97 / 16 |
| excerpts, tuning (150) | 93 | **94** | 112 → 113 |

- **Repeatability.** Repeated runs of the current layout were identical row for row, but for one index held-out row (hq-0045) in one run of four: a score tie broken by `updated_at`, which seeding stamps at wall-clock time. The prototype's three index runs of each set agree but for the same row, which was delivered in one held-out run of three (258). Over seven runs of the new layout the index held-out total is 257 five times and 258 twice — hq-0045 once and hq-0046 once, both short-term ties at the excerpt cut broken the same way *(rev 3.40; the second review, F5)*.
- **Rev 3.37's changes move no proxy row.** The script-boundary split, the body table confined to unsegmented terms, the exact CJK and stop-word terms, and the trigger give the same rows as rev 3.36's prototype, but for hq-0045. The whole-word query runs only at the cap, which 83 notes never reach.
- **One total falls: a net loss of one row, for the lead to rule on.**
  - Five questions lose a relied-on note in some run (eight rows over the four runs), and eleven gain one.
  - Seven of the eight totals rise or hold, including the held-out gate set's: +3 / 0 in index mode, +2 in excerpts mode.
  - Index tuning's listed-or-delivered falls from 95 to 94, in every run on both sides. The row is tq-0032, below.
  - Rev 3.36 called the totals "no net loss". On that one total it is a loss, and LQ17's rule makes a net loss a stop, so the revision goes to the lead with the row.
  - The one variant then measured that recovered it (E4, §6.5.8) gave back more on the held-out questions.
  - **The lead's ruling, 2026-09-27: tq-0032 is accepted.** The held-out gate set shows no net loss (index +3 / 0, excerpts +2), and the only variant that recovered tq-0032 (E4) cost the held-out set one listed and three delivered rows (rev 3.41). *(Measured after the ruling, 2026-09-27, by the close-out's B2 (below): the five header-relevance variants recover tq-0032 too, and each costs the held-out set at least one row — excerpts held-out 288 → 287 in every one — so the ruling's premise holds for every variant that recovered it; rev 3.42.)*
- **Every lost row, explained** from both layouts' rankings:
  - **hq-0131** (both modes), note "Lantern 本地环境". The question's Chinese words match nothing. The 24 notes that mention Lantern share its one matching term, `lantern`, and BM25 detail orders the tie.
  - **hq-0152 and hq-0228** (excerpts), note "No analytics and no comments on Moss". The question (`visitor numbers`, `访问统计`) paraphrases a word the note does not contain, so it ties on `moss` with the other notes that mention Moss. It was 8th of 8 in that tie, within 0.1 of the notes around it, and is now 9th.
  - **hq-0318** (both modes), note "Python tooling: uv and ruff". It loses exactly the trigram weight, 1.0. The `dependencies` in its body had earned the best trigram BM25 on top of the word table's BM25 for the same whole word.
  - **tq-0032** (both modes), note "Editor: Helix". The question's distinctive term, `hx`, is two Latin characters, and a term that short is dropped when longer terms exist (§7c Q9, unchanged). What remains is `use`, a substring of `uses`, `used` and `user` in 27 of the 83 notes. Before, this note's `uses` and `used` won the trigram BM25. Now `use*` earns the word table's BM25, where a title counts four times a body, so titles that begin "The user's" win. The note falls from 5th to 11th, out of the ten lines.
- **The gained rows** are the same mechanism in reverse, plus prefix hits (hq-0381: `book` finds `booking`).
- **Variants measured and not taken.** Each is given as the rows with every relied-on note delivered, in the order index held-out / excerpts held-out / index tuning / excerpts tuning; the current layout is 254 / 286 / 82 / 93.
  - One trigram table, with a prefix on every literal: 252 / 283 / 81 / 91.
  - One trigram table with exact literals: 255 / 284 / 82 / 91.
  - No prefix at all: 247 / 283 / 81 / 91.
  - Prefixes only from 4 characters: 250 / 284 / 82 / 91.
  - Two trigram tables, with the body table ranking every term: 256 / 289 / 82 / 93. It lost tq-0073 to the bias described in §6.5.2.
  - A prefix hit's relevance in the trigram slot (E4): 254 / 288 / 82 / 93, with listed-or-delivered at 292 and 95 (§6.5.8).

**The scale proxy.** The proxy's questions are searched at the index level (automatic mode, the recall's 20 candidates, and no truncation, so each rank is read off the whole ranking).
